## Y. The input seam under a two-player game (2026-07-28)

Found by working `docs/planning/engine/netcode.md`'s **"Next online slice"**
after Campaign 1 closed. Step 1 (quarantine external effects) is done, step 3
(matchbox) is ⛔ deferred until Smash by Jon's order, so step 2 — *"a two-peer
native/loopback GGRS acceptance test"* — is the live one. Reading for it turned
up two things, and neither is the test.

- ✔ **Y1 ROLLBACK COVERS ONE SEAT, and the game now seats four.** CLOSED 2026-07-28.
  `build_sync_test_session` is `with_num_players(1)` + `add_player(Local, 0)`,
  and `publish_ggrs_input` takes `inputs.first()` into the singular
  `ControlFrame`. Seats 1–3 never enter GGRS at all:
  `populate_secondary_slot_controls` writes straight into `SlotControls[N]`, and
  says so in its own doc comment — *"that resource is the primary seat's"*.
  **This is not a bug in couch play.** Local play predicts nothing, so a seat
  outside the rollback system is invisible and harmless. It is the precise,
  nameable gap between "rollback works" (proved, repeatedly, for one input
  stream) and "rollback MULTIPLAYER works", which is the headline feature the
  netcode doc is about — and C4 shipped a 2–4 player versus mode this week, so
  the oracle now covers a quarter of what the game seats.
  ⚠ `two_ggrs_harnesses_match_under_the_same_input_stream` already exists, so
  the doc's "two independent harnesses" verification is NOT the gap. The gap is
  two PLAYERS in one session.
  ✔ **the PLUMBING landed 2026-07-28.** `SyncTestSettings.players` (clamped to
  the slots the game supports, defaulting to 1 so every existing caller means
  what it meant), the builder loops `add_player`, `PendingSeatInputs` carries
  seats 1.., `capture_latched_local_input` drains their latches on the same edge
  seat zero's is drained, `publish_local_inputs` gives each handle ITS OWN frame,
  and `publish_ggrs_input` routes handle 0 → `ControlFrame` and handles 1.. →
  `SlotControls`, which is where `tick_player_brains` already looks.
  ⛔ **the defect this removes, precisely:** `publish_local_inputs` inserted seat
  zero's `pending.0` for EVERY handle. Correct while there was exactly one and
  silently wrong the moment there were two — four pads driving one input stream,
  and a sync test checksum-comparing a simulation nobody is playing.
  RED-PROBED: restoring that line fails `each_local_handle_submits_its_own_input_stream`.
  ⚠ **`publish_ggrs_input` is what puts seats 1.. INSIDE rollback.** Before it
  they were written on the FEEL clock by the host's own input path and GGRS never
  saw them, so a resimulated frame replayed seat zero faithfully and gave every
  other seat whatever the device happened to be doing at replay time.
  → 5 tests; 29 rollback oracles stay green.
  ✔ **the ACCEPTANCE half landed too: `two_seats_drive_independent_streams_through_a_rewind`.**
  `with_rollback_players(2)` on the harness, `drive_seat` as the seam a driver
  writes a secondary stream through (the twin of `drive_control_frame`, and it
  exists for the same reason: every driver that grew its own copy grew the same
  bug), and the two seats push OPPOSITE directions for 32 frames through the
  real save/rewind/resimulate loop. That is netcode.md's step 2.
  ⛔ **the test caught a bug I had just written**, which is the best argument for
  having written it: `drive_control_frame` cleared `PendingSeatInputs`. Since
  `drive_seat` is called BEFORE the step it applies to, the seam was built and
  then emptied by its own sibling one line later. A driver that wants a seat
  neutral drives it neutral; silence is not a request.
  ⛔ **and the FIRST version of the assertion was too weak — it passed against
  the original defect.** `seat_two.axis_x != 0.0` is satisfied when handle 1 is
  handed seat ZERO's frame, because that frame also has a non-zero axis. The
  claim has to be that the streams DIFFER, which is why the seats are driven in
  opposite directions: the assertion is now on the SIGN, and it fails against the
  old `publish_local_inputs` with "seat two is pushing the same way as seat one".
  ⚠ this is the third time today an instrument was upgraded and its comment got
  confident in the same commit (S1's lesson). The difference here is the red
  probe, which is the only thing that tells the two apart.
  ⛔ **and the EXTERNAL-CONSUMER job caught the API break, exactly as its own doc
  comment says it exists to.** Adding `players` to `SyncTestSettings` — a public
  struct with public fields — breaks every out-of-tree struct literal. Twenty of
  twenty-one jobs stayed green; `cargo check --workspace` cannot see the
  fixture's separate workspace, so nothing in-repo noticed.
  → the fixture now uses `..Default::default()`, which is what a third party
  should have been doing: name what you care about, not every field.
  ▢ **the API question, written down rather than answered:** a public-field
  settings struct breaks consumers on every internal addition. `#[non_exhaustive]`
  is the standard answer and it forbids external struct literals ENTIRELY, even
  with functional-update syntax — so it needs a builder or constructor first,
  which is a real design choice and not a mechanical one. Pre-release means no
  compat tax, so breaking is allowed; it should still be DELIBERATE, and today's
  was not.

- ✔ **Y2 player two's short taps could be DROPPED. Fixed 2026-07-28.**
  Same doc comment, stated as a known limit: the primary seat's frame passes
  through `ControlFrameLatch`, which ORs sub-tick press edges together so a tap
  between two ticks is never swallowed. **A secondary seat has no latch.** On a
  fixed-tick host a very short player-two tap is missed.
  This is a FAIRNESS asymmetry in the couch versus mode — two people on two pads,
  and one of them has forgiving input and the other does not. It was written down
  as bounded and named rather than hidden, which is right, and it has been true
  since the second seat landed.
  ⚠ it interacts with the round-start countdown landed today: the count clears
  buffered edges on the transition to `Fighting`, so the very first frames of a
  round are exactly where a dropped tap is most likely and most visible.
  → `SlotControlLatches` — one `ControlFrameLatch` per slot, folded on the FEEL
  clock by `populate_secondary_slot_controls` and drained on the TICK clock by
  `publish_latched_slot_controls`. The primary seat's two-system shape, installed
  under the same `sim_is_fixed_tick()` condition and ordered the same way,
  because a frame-stepped host has one frame per tick and nothing to bridge.
  ⚠ **slot 0 is deliberately skipped** — it already drains `ControlFrameLatch`,
  and latching it twice would hold one press across two ticks. A test asserts
  seat two's edge does NOT appear in slot 0 for exactly that reason.
  ⚠ **a pause CLEARS rather than drains.** `take` retains levels by design; a
  seat that has stopped being driven must not hand a held direction to the tick
  after the pause, so the not-gameplay branch calls `reset`. Same rule the
  primary seat already followed.
  ⚠ **the sample no longer writes `SlotControls` directly under fixed tick** —
  doing both would be the sample racing its own latch, and the tick would see
  whichever system ran last.
  → three tests: the sub-tick tap survives, seats do not leak into each other,
  and a reset drops held levels rather than only edges. 24/24 versus tests stay
  green, including the four-pad one.
  ⛔ **and the fix opened a rollback hazard that the fix then had to close.**
  The drain runs in the SIM schedule, which under a rollback host IS the GGRS
  schedule — so a resimulated tick would drain the latch again, find it empty,
  and give the seat neutral input on the replayed tick but not the original. A
  desync the simulation manufactures itself. It is skipped on a replay pass now
  (`SimulationReplayState`, the fact that already exists for exactly this).
  ⚠ **the primary seat has no equivalent hazard, and the reason is Y1.** It
  drains too, and GGRS then OVERWRITES `ControlFrame` from the session's
  confirmed inputs — so the drain is harmless there. Seats 1-3 are not in the
  session at all, so for them the drain IS the value and had to be guarded
  instead. The asymmetry in the fix is the same asymmetry Y1 describes.

## Z. The verification tool cannot see what a stranger sees (2026-07-28)

- ▢ **Z1 `capture_scene` captures ROOMS, not ROUTES — and silently.** Found by
  taking `headless-verification.md` at its word (*"always draw blind work should
  produce an image rather than assert it cannot"*) and trying to photograph a day
  of title-screen and versus work. Asking for `versus_arena` — a shell-route
  experience with its own prepared session — captured the default SANDBOX room
  instead. No error, no warning; the PNG just shows somewhere else, which is the
  worst failure mode a verification tool has.
  ⛔ **the surfaces a stranger sees FIRST are exactly the ones it cannot reach:**
  the launcher, the startup cards, the versus stage and its HUD are all routes.
  Today's text-size fix, hover, countdown card and per-seat gauges all shipped
  without an image, and the reason was never "headless cannot see UI" —
  `--include-ui` works fine — it is that the tool only knows rooms.
  → the fix: a `--route <SHELL_ROUTE_ID>` mode composing the shell host
  (`build_visible_app(NoWindow, ..)`, which every rendered test already uses),
  settling to the route, and handing the same offscreen target to the same
  readback. **The readback half is done and route-agnostic**; only the app build
  differs, so this is smaller than it sounds.
  ⚠ and it should FAIL LOUDLY on an unknown room or route rather than falling
  back — a capture that silently photographs somewhere else is how a blind agent
  reports the wrong thing with confidence.
  ⚠ **ATTEMPTED 2026-07-28 and REVERTED. Three hypotheses eliminated; the
  remaining question is named.** A `--route` mode that always errors is a trap,
  so the code came back out — but the dead ends are worth more than the diff:
  1. **The shell app has no loop runner.** `build_visible_app(NoWindow, ..)` is
     stepped by hand in every rendered test, so `app.run()` executes ONE update,
     never reaches the warmup frame, and exits having written nothing. Silently —
     the same failure this mode exists to remove, one level down. Drive it with a
     manual `update()` loop.
  2. **`MainCamera`/`FrontHudCamera` is not enough.** Those are GAMEPLAY cameras
     and a shell route has neither, so `setup_capture_target` builds an image no
     camera draws into. Retargeting every camera works: the launcher reports
     **3 cameras**.
  3. **And it STILL does not complete.** With all three retargeted and the app
     stepped 600 times, the readback is REQUESTED and `ReadbackComplete` never
     fires. So the blocker is neither the runner nor the camera targeting: it is
     the render-app GPU readback path under a manually-stepped visible app.

  ✔ **ANSWERED 2026-07-28, and the answer is that hypothesis 3 was chasing
  something impossible.** `VisibleRenderMode::NoWindow` sets

      RenderPlugin { render_creation: Automatic(WgpuSettings { backends: None }) }

  and the line above it says what that means: *"`backends: None` omits the
  RenderApp; disable plugins whose only job is to register extraction/render
  work there"* — which is why `CorePipelinePlugin` and `GizmoRenderPlugin` are
  disabled right beside it. **There is no render app, so there is no render
  graph, so a readback can never complete.** Not slow, not mis-targeted:
  absent. Every camera retarget in hypothesis 2 was aiming at an image nothing
  could ever draw into.

  → **so the fix is not "make readback work under NoWindow", it is a THIRD
  render mode.** `capture_scene` already proves the recipe for a working
  offscreen GPU app: real `DefaultPlugins` with a real wgpu backend,
  `primary_window: None`, `WinitPlugin` disabled, and `ScheduleRunnerPlugin` as
  the loop runner. What it does NOT do is compose the shell. The shape is one
  more arm in `build_visible_app`'s `match render` that keeps the shell
  composition and swaps the render settings — the two halves already exist, in
  two files, and have never been put together.
  ✔ **SOLVED 2026-07-28. `capture_scene --route <id>` photographs a shell route
  through the composition a player runs.** Three more hypotheses beyond the
  three already recorded, each of which looked like the answer:

  4. **`NoWindow` has no render app** (`backends: None`), so the readback could
     never complete. Fixed by a THIRD mode, `VisibleRenderMode::OffscreenGpu` —
     the same recipe with a real wgpu adapter, `ScheduleRunnerPlugin` as the
     loop runner, and the FULL plugin set including the LDtk tile spine, which
     `NoWindow` must skip because tilemap needs a render app.
  5. **Cameras do not exist at `Startup`.** A route spawns them when it
     composes, several frames in, so the marker-keyed retarget adopted ZERO and
     produced a blank image that was written successfully and reported as a
     capture — the same silent-wrong-answer, three levels down. Adoption runs
     every frame now, and the count is printed when it CHANGES.
  6. **Every camera clears.** Point a route's stack (world, UI, overlay) at one
     image with default clear configs and each wipes the one before it; the file
     that lands is whatever the LAST camera drew over a fresh clear. Ordered by
     `Camera::order`, only the lowest clears, the rest overlay.

  ✔ **and the FIRST photograph earned the whole detour by finding two defects
  no test had:**
  - the **"Ambition" title overlaps the first menu row** rather than sitting
    above it — a layout bug nothing asserts, because no assertion knows what
    "above" looks like;
  - a **touch D-PAD is drawn on the title screen of a desktop build.** Today's
    earlier fix hid the gameplay ACTION BUTTONS while a menu owns input, on the
    argument that a verb nobody can press must not be on screen. The STICK was
    left out, and the rendered test that covers this queries
    `TouchActionButton` — which the stick is not. Fixed by gating
    `TouchSurface::Movement` on the same `gameplay_owned` rule, deliberately not
    on the shared `MobileTouchUiRoot`, because the action bezel carries Start
    and Reset and hiding their root takes a phone's way out with it.

  ▢ **the title/menu overlap is NOT fixed** — it is a layout decision about a
  screen Jon has opinions on, and it is now visible rather than invisible.
  ✔ kept from the attempt: `ShellRouteCatalog::ids()`, so a refusal can NAME the
  routes that exist. "Unknown route" is a puzzle; "unknown route, here are the
  eight" is a typo somebody can fix without a debugger.

## F. The blast zone the engine kept promising (2026-07-28)

Track F (`docs/planning/demos/super-smash-siblings.md`) lists "blast zones = the
world-AABB OOB event | landed" in its CONSUMES table, so the check before
building was: does that event exist? The answer took four greps and turned into
the biggest single finding of the day.

**Three doc comments promise the same capability, and it did not exist.**
`DeathPolicy::Unbounded` — smash percent, the meter that never kills — says
"death comes from the WORLD — the blast-zone / OOB / fell-out gate the engine
already owns". `kills_at_max` says "the blast-zone gate owns its death".
`actor_hit.rs` says it a third time. The gate is real and computes `fell_out`
correctly, gravity-relative and all. What it then did was merge that fact into
one anonymous `hazard` bool shared with spike contact — and the death publisher
downstream *documented the consequence in a comment*: "the kernel gate does not
distinguish spikes from the void, and neither does any consumer today". Both
halves true, the second BECAUSE of the first. `DeathPolicy::Unbounded` had zero
production callers, which is what a death policy looks like when the death it
depends on cannot be observed.

- ✔ **F0a** `FrameEvents.reset: Option<ResetCause>` (Requested / Hazard /
  Drowned / LeftTheWorld) replaces the `reset`+`hazard` bool pair;
  `PlayerBodyFrameOutput.reset: Option<BodyReset>` replaces the
  `reset: bool` + `reset_origin: Option<Vec2>` pair that had to agree with
  nothing making it. `HitSource::LeftTheWorld` names the void at the death seam.
  Red-probed three ways.
- ✔ **F0b** the "ONE hazard/out-of-bounds gate every policy publishes through"
  was two: the axis-swept policy kept a private transcription, literal `200.0`
  and all. Now one function, three callers, and a test that drives all three
  motion policies past the same edge. That literal was also the only thing
  deciding where a world ends, so no stage could disagree with it — it is now
  `World::blast_margin`, serde-defaulted.
- ✔ **F0c** an actor that left the world **fell forever**: its frame events were
  read for presentation and dropped, actors are never teleported to the player
  spawn, and the promised "actor's damage / OOB systems" do not exist. Nothing
  anywhere culled a fallen actor (only projectiles and pickups are culled by
  position). It now writes a lethal `HitTarget::Actor` hit on the channel the
  shark-crash already uses, which buys the whole existing death pipeline —
  `RulesetOwnsDeath`, respawn policy, banner, death cue, `ActorDiedMessage`.
  The blast zone kills regardless of `kills_at_max`, which is the other half of
  what `Unbounded` means.
- ✔ **F0d** the versus arena was a closed box — floor plus two walls — so there
  was nowhere to be knocked TO, and the only way to lose was to run out of
  health. It is now a main platform in open space with two recovery ledges, no
  walls, and a 96px blast margin. No new rule was needed: a KO zeroes health,
  and `round_result` already scored on health.

### The authoring toolchain was quietly broken (found registering the field def)

Adding the `blast_margin` level field meant running the LDtk tools, which meant
running their tests, which is how a second unrun Python suite turned up.

- ✔ **F0i** `tools/ambition_ldtk_tools/tests` had **11 of 149 RED on a clean
  tree**, and the runner's `repo tooling` job only ever ran `scripts/tests`.
  Every failure was the same cause: asset paths that moved out of
  `crates/ambition_actors/assets` and were never re-pointed. The suite was not
  testing the tools; it was describing where the repo used to be.
- ✔ **F0j** and it was not only the tests. **15 CLI modules** defaulted `--ldtk`
  to the same dead path, each spelling it out by hand — so `ldtk edit measure`,
  `set-field`, `gates`, `portals`, `snap-to-floor` and ten more crashed with
  `FileNotFoundError` the moment you omitted `--ldtk`. Verified against HEAD
  before the fix, not inferred. `ldtk/paths.py` has held the correct answer the
  whole time, and its own docstring says it exists "so individual commands do
  not recreate stale repository-layout assumptions" — which 15 commands, and
  the tests, then did. There were THREE different wrong spellings in play,
  including a `crates/ambition_content/...` that has never been a real
  directory. All of them now call `default_sandbox_ldtk()`.
- ✔ **F0k** the suite runs it now: `ldtk authoring tools` is a backbone job, so
  the toolchain every room in the game is authored through cannot rot silently
  again. This is the second time this week the fix was "run the tests that
  already existed".

### Still open

- ▢ **F0e** `DeathPolicy::Unbounded` is now *reachable* and still has no
  production caller. Selecting it for the duelists turns versus from HP
  depletion into pure knock-off, which is the genre — but only if knockback
  actually launches far enough to reach a blast zone. **Measured, and it does
  not.** A clean forward smash (the duelists' authored 420px/s launch) landed on
  a fighter standing on the platform's lip moves them **15.5px horizontally and
  18.8px vertically**, with a peak speed of exactly 420px/s. The launch IS
  applied — the peak proves it — and then something removes it inside about two
  frames. 420px/s sustained for the half-second window would be ~210px; it
  travelled 7% of that.

  Two things this cost, worth recording because both are the same mistake:
  the FIRST measurement reported the four-second maximum displacement and got
  `dx = -295px` — the CPU walking home to the middle of the stage. A knockback
  measurement has to end before the victim gets its steering back, or it
  measures the brain. And the number to watch was never "how far" but "does it
  leave the ground": knockback resolves to roughly `(322, -269)` here, so the
  upward component is real and 18.8px of rise means it is being eaten, not
  omitted.

  So F0e is NOT a tuning row. Making the number bigger multiplies something that
  is being discarded. Find what discards it first.

  **Found, and it is the fourth instance today of the same shape.** The per-frame
  trace shows `vel.x` falling by exactly 62.5px/s every frame — a constant
  3750px/s², which is `AIR_STOP_ASSIST` to the digit. That is the airborne
  hands-off deceleration, and it decays a body toward `carried_run`, the
  "carried-momentum floor". `carried_run`'s own doc says the floor exists so
  coasting "must still not bleed away speed the WORLD imparted (**portal flings,
  knockback**)". Knockback is named there. It was never wired: the portal transit
  adapter is the ONLY writer of `carried_run` in the repo, so a launched body's
  floor is zero and the launch is erased in seven frames.

  A mechanism exists, its documentation names the exact case, and nothing
  connects the case to the mechanism — the same sentence as
  `DeathPolicy::Unbounded`, as the "ONE gate every policy publishes through",
  and as `RoomMetadata::mode`.

  **Tried, measured, and NOT landed.** Setting `carried_run` to the launch's
  side component — the way the portal fling does — works exactly as intended in
  isolation: same smash, same fighter, same lip, `|dx|` 15.5px → **111.8px**,
  and they leave the stage. The per-frame trace is the whole story — `vel.x`
  used to fall 62.5/frame to zero by t+7 and instead holds flat at 329.7 while
  gravity does its work on `vel.y` alone.

  It is a CORE FEEL change touching every struck body, not just fighters, so it
  went to the full suite. The suite found two things, and the first was a real
  defect in the change itself.

  ✔ **The defect (mine), found by reading rather than by the suite.** An actor
  killed by the blast zone ran the ordinary death path, and
  `RespawnPolicy::InPlace` respawns a body AT ITS CURRENT POSITION — which, for
  a body that just left the world, is still outside the world. It dies again on
  the next tick, respawns again, and the room acquires a body whose entire
  behaviour is dying. `sandbag_finite` is the one archetype that authors
  `InPlace`, so it is the one archetype for which "gone" and "the authored
  policy" disagree — and it is now the subject of the test.

  I first blamed this for the oracle's stalled clock. It is NOT: applying the
  fix left the oracle's numbers byte-identical (300 / 59 / 64), which is what
  disproved it. Worth recording, because a plausible cause that changes nothing
  is the cheapest kind of wrong answer to keep believing.

  Landed as `KillDisposition` — a named decision (`RespawnInPlace` / `Defeated`
  / `GoneFromTheWorld`) rather than an inference from the order of an
  `if`-chain, red-probed by deleting the `LeftTheWorld` arm. Gone means gone:
  no respawn timer, and no bounty coin or death explosion either, since those
  would land in the void where no player can walk.

  ✔ Also landed: the blast hit is gated on the body being ALIVE. The gate is a
  position test that re-fires every tick, so a corpse outside the world was
  writing a lethal hit per frame that the damage pass silently refused — a
  per-frame cost with no visible symptom, which is worse than a visible bug.

  ⛔ **AND THE FIX IS NOT LANDED.** Reverted. The oracle's route still completes
  (all four events by frame 164, same ~5.7s wall clock as clean), but GGRS
  advances **300 times in 600 harness steps** where a clean tree advances more
  than 600. Isolated to the single `*carried_run = …` line by disabling just
  that line and watching the oracle go green — so the line is certainly the
  cause.

  ⛔ **MY EXPLANATION OF WHY WAS WRONG, and measuring it is what showed that.**
  I wrote here that conserving the launch pins a struck body against its
  attacker, so it is re-hit as its i-frames lapse, and the resulting hitstop —
  a GLOBAL clock rule, `scale = 0.0` whenever the PRIMARY player's
  `hitstop_timer` is live — starves the simulation clock. Plausible, and the
  clock rule is real (`emit_player_time_intent_system`). But instrumenting the
  oracle to count body-frames of each reaction timer says the opposite:

  | | hitstop | hitstun | i-frames | result |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|---|
  | clean | 70 | 96 | 496 | passes |
  | carried floor | **47** | **85** | **381** | fails |

  Hitstop went DOWN by a third. FEWER hits land, not more — carried momentum
  carries a struck body AWAY from what hit it, which is the opposite of
  pinning. So the slowdown is not hitstop, and the mechanism is still unknown.

  What is known: both runs reach `last_simulated_frame: 64` with `load_runs:
  59`, and the advance count differs by almost exactly 2×. Same frames reached,
  same rollbacks issued, half the schedule runs. That smells discrete rather
  than continuous — a stepping or accumulator difference — and it is where the
  next person should start rather than re-deriving the hitstop story.

  ⚠ Recorded at length because a plausible cause that survives in a document is
  worse than no cause at all: it is what the next reader builds on.

  What the fix NEEDS is a release condition: the floor is momentum you were
  given while you could not act, so it must be surrendered the moment you can.
  Bounding it to the recoil-lock window is the shape, and it needs plumbing the
  two damage callers do not have today (`BodyCombat::decay_reaction_timers` is
  shared but never sees the flight cluster). Do NOT simply re-apply the line;
  the pinning is a real gameplay defect and would ship with it.

  Landed from this investigation, because both are correct with or without the
  carry: the OOB in-place respawn loop above, and the alive-gate on the blast
  hit.

  ⚠ **The duel-arena assertion (not mine, but exposed).** `duel_arena` asserted
  "at least one fighter opens a regroup-dash burst", labelled a proof that the
  dash intent resolves through the shared body pipeline. It was a bet on AI
  CADENCE, and its own comment recorded the bet losing twice — per-fighter
  weakened to aggregate, then the robot's contribution going to zero. Measured
  on a clean tree, the whole assertion rested on ONE fighter's **18 dash frames
  out of 600**. Knockback carrying moved every number in the fight (PCA fly
  666→395, shield 179→398) and those 18 went to 0; both fighters now regroup by
  flying, which the comment already conceded is valid regrouping, and flight IS
  asserted per fighter. What the assertion claimed to prove is proven directly
  in `dash_tests` — same intent, real integration, dasher must cover more
  ground — which does not care what mood the AI is in.

  Left STANDING for now, because the change that exposed it was reverted and a
  guard should not be removed on the strength of a diff that is not landing.
  It should go when the carry lands, and this row is the argument for it.
### Opened by closing F0f (2026-07-28)

- ✔/▢ **F0m** — the row's premise was wrong, and checking it was worth more
  than doing it. I had the Sanic speedway down as the obvious first customer
  for a side blast zone. It is not: the speedway is 6400px of course and its
  right edge is the END of it. Killing there would delete the finish line. A
  side blast zone is a FIGHTING-stage concept, and every shipped room in this
  repo is a platformer room, so "no shipped level authors one" is the correct
  state and not a gap. The first real customer is the SSB demo (Track F §5,
  which now names the three field ids), and inventing a use before then would
  be worse than waiting.

  ✔ What WAS worth doing is the guard the row led to: every level field the
  ENGINE reads must be DECLARED by every project
  (`tools/ambition_ldtk_tools/tests/test_level_field_defs.py`). An undeclared
  field is a channel nobody can author, and it fails SILENTLY — the reader
  returns `None`, the composer takes its default, the room looks fine. That is
  exactly how `mode` sat unauthorable for months.

  It found one on its first run: `gallery` — the flag that switches a room to
  Hall-of-Characters behaviour — was undeclared in `intro.ldtk` and
  `you_have_to_cut_the_rope.ldtk`. Neither needs it today, which is the same
  argument that let `mode` rot until someone needed it. Declared in both now,
  and 154 tool tests green.

- ✔ **F0n** the blast margins are visible. `draw_blast_zones` rides
  `show_room_bounds` — a room's outer edge and its kill line are the same idea
  one step apart — and draws the fall boundary always, the side and ceiling
  boundaries only when the stage opted in. An ABSENT line is the honest picture
  of a direction that does not kill, and the two colours say which is which:
  the unconditional one reads as the default, the opt-in pair as choices this
  stage made.

  The geometry is a pure `blast_zone_lines(world, gravity_dir) ->
  Vec<BlastZoneLine>` separate from the drawing, because a gizmo call cannot be
  asserted and a segment can. Three tests, all red-probed: drawing in screen
  space (the trap — the gate measures along the body's `down`, so under
  rightward gravity the pit boundary is VERTICAL and a `y = size.y + margin`
  line would show an author a boundary the simulation does not use), and
  drawing the opt-in lines unconditionally.

- ✔/▢ **F0p** the duplication is gone at both sites that had it.

  ✔ The reader had three copies of `field_i32(name).filter(|px| *px >= 0)`,
  sitting next to a `take_count` closure that already did exactly that job.
  One `take_px` closure now, with the reasoning in one place: zero is
  MEANINGFUL ("you are out the instant you cross my edge" is a legitimate
  stage), negatives are REFUSED rather than clamped, because a clamp turns an
  authoring mistake into a room that merely behaves oddly. Both halves are
  red-probed — `map(|px| px.max(0))` and `filter(> 0)` each fail their own
  case — and the negative test now drives all three fields, since a regression
  in a shared closure is invisible if only one field exercises it.

  ✔ The composer forwarded three fields in two calls. `with_blast_zones(fall,
  side, ceiling)` replaces `with_blast_margin` + `with_optional_blast_zones`
  there, because a forwarding site that applies margins one at a time is a site
  that can forget one, and a forgotten margin is SILENT — the field keeps its
  default and the room looks fine.

  ▢ What is NOT done is the struct. `RoomMetadata` still carries the three as
  flat fields and `World` mirrors them flat, which is honest while there are
  three; grouping them would want `World`, `RoomMetadata`, the serde defaults
  and the merge to move together for a payoff that is mostly aesthetic today.
  The trigger to do it is a FOURTH direction (a per-face margin, or a
  per-blast-zone respawn delay) — at four, the flat list stops being a list and
  starts being a struct someone forgot to write.

### Found by asking what a KO sounds like (2026-07-28)

- ✔ **F0q every versus KO was SILENT, and so was every blast-zone death.** The
  death drama — the spark burst, the ragdoll debris, and `SfxMessage::Death` in
  the body's OWN voice — lived inside the exploration DEFEAT arm of
  `apply_actor_hit`, past two `else if`s. So a body whose death a RULESET owns
  got none of it, and every fighter in a versus round carries
  `RulesetOwnsDeath`. The mode I spent today giving blast zones to announced its
  own payoff with nothing but a health bar reaching zero.

  The separation the arms are actually for is the ECONOMY, and that arm's own
  comment says so: "no bounty coin, no heart, no death explosion, no split
  offspring, no held-item drop, no in-place respawn timer". All economy. A body
  dying in its own voice was never on that list; it was sharing a block with the
  coins by accident of nesting.

  Hoisted to run for every `killed` body. The arms keep the economy, which is
  what they were written to decide.

  ⚠ **and the SAME defect was one file away, on the player's path.** Seat 1 is
  an actor and dies through `apply_actor_hit`; seat 0 is the adopted
  `PlayerEntity` and dies through the player damage drain, whose
  `ruleset_owns_death` arm returned `false` and emitted nothing at all. So the
  HUMAN's own knockout was the quietest event in the mode. Fixed the same way
  and for the same reason: a ruleset owns where the body goes, what it costs
  and who scores it — none of which is the sound of losing.

  Both halves are one test, driving the two paths separately, red-probed by
  putting the drama back inside the exploration arm.

### The stage, drawn (2026-07-28)

- ✔ **F0r** [diagrams/versus-arena-2026-07-28.svg](diagrams/versus-arena-2026-07-28.svg)
  — the arena's authored geometry with all three blast boundaries, every number
  read from `versus.rs` and `seating.rs` rather than eyeballed.

  It is a DIAGRAM, not a screenshot, and the distinction is the honest part: the
  versus room is a Rust `RoomSpec` registered under the versus experience, so
  `capture_scene` cannot reach it (that is Z1, three hypotheses deep). Drawing
  the authored numbers is what can be done truthfully from here; claiming a
  render would not be.

  It earns its place by making two things reviewable that no test can judge:
  the recovery ledges sit at y=304, 96px above the main platform, which may be
  too high to be a real second chance; and a fighter leaving the platform falls
  **236px** before the floor zone takes them, which may read as slow. Both are
  feel calls, both are now visible, and neither was visible this morning.

### Two numbers that are Jon's, not mine

Both became MEANINGFUL today for the first time, and neither is a bug. Naming
them here rather than picking them, because each changes what the game IS.

- ✔ **F0e — knockback carries its launch, for exactly as long as the body cannot
  act.** The row's own measurement: a clean 420px/s forward smash moved a fighter
  **15.5px**. The launch WAS applied — peak speed was exactly 420 — and
  `AIR_STOP_ASSIST` removed it inside about two frames, because the hands-off stop
  assist decays toward `carried_run` and the portal adapter was the only writer
  that field had ever had. Its doc names knockback as one of the two cases it
  exists for.
  → the first attempt set `carried_run` and was REVERTED. This one adds
  `BodyFlightState::carried_hold`: the window the carry is owed for, counted down
  in the integrator — the one function that already runs every tick with `dt` for
  every body, so the release cannot drift from a watcher somewhere else. Set at
  the ONE post-hit launch site to the hitstun duration, and rollback-serialised
  beside the value it guards.
  ⚠ **and it must ZERO the floor when the window ends, not hand it to
  `carried_decay`.** That rate is `0.0` on the axis-swept profile — the floor
  never bleeds — so "expire into the ordinary decay" is no expiry at all, and a
  body hit once would coast at its launch speed for the rest of its life. That is
  the core-feel change that got the first attempt reverted. Zeroing the FLOOR is
  not braking: `kin_vel` is untouched, so the launched body flies on and
  decelerates under the ordinary stop assist, which is what should happen once it
  can act.
  ⚠ **hitstun, not the recoil lock.** The recoil lock is the short hard beat at
  the front; hitstun is the whole span with no authority over the trajectory, and
  "momentum you were given while you could not act" is a statement about
  authority.
  → ✔ **the rollback exit oracle is GREEN**, which is what reverted the last
  attempt. I did not prove why the unbounded version was red; bounding it made
  the question moot, and that is recorded rather than dressed up as a diagnosis.
  → `a_launched_body_keeps_its_launch_for_the_window_it_cannot_act` and
  `the_carried_launch_is_surrendered_when_the_window_ends`. The first probe —
  removing the carry — reproduces the original defect independently at **20.1px
  against the 100.8px the launch describes**, the same order as the 15.5px
  measured end-to-end. The second probe is the reverted shape, an unbounded
  carry.
  → ✔ **the `duel_arena` dash assertion is DELETED, on the argument this row wrote
  on a clean tree** so it could not be mistaken for a test removed to make a
  change pass: it was a bet on AI cadence, its own comment recorded the bet losing
  twice, and measured on a clean tree the whole thing rested on ONE fighter's 18
  dash frames out of 600. Both fighters now regroup by flying, which its comment
  already conceded is valid regrouping, and `fly_frames > 0` is asserted PER
  fighter. What it claimed to prove is proven directly, without an opinion about
  the AI's mood, by `dash_tests::a_dash_capable_body_covers_more_ground_than_a_
  walker_over_the_window`.
  → ✔ **and the reset test's ratio was brittleness one level down.** `after_gap <
  roamed * 0.3` failed at 2px from a 427px spawn because the fighter had only
  roamed 5px: a denominator that can be tiny makes a ratio STRICTER than any fixed
  tolerance. It now passes on the ratio or on a generous absolute floor.
  ▢ what is NOT done: `DeathPolicy::Unbounded` is still uncalled, so this makes
  knock-offs REACHABLE without deciding that they are the win condition. That is
  F0-J2, and it is Jon's.

- ⊘ **F0-J1 — directional influence.** *(Jon's number; collected in [awaiting-maintainer-decision.md](awaiting-maintainer-decision.md))* `di_adjust` is landed and pure, and
  `di_max_angle` is `0.0` everywhere; the combat-model table says outright
  "turning DI on for a fighter is a feel number **Jon sets**" and suggests
  ≈0.31 rad / 18°. Until today there was no blast zone to be launched into, so
  the number could not matter. Now a launch can end a stock, and DI is exactly
  the difference between a knock-off that is a coin flip and one that is a
  read. The wiring is done; only the number is missing.
- ⊘ **F0-J2 — does versus end on HP or on stocks?** *(Jon's call; collected in [awaiting-maintainer-decision.md](awaiting-maintainer-decision.md))* `DeathPolicy::Unbounded`
  works now and is still uncalled. Leaving it uncalled means versus has TWO win
  conditions — drain them, or throw them out — which is a defensible place to
  stand and is what ships today. Selecting it makes damage purely a knockback
  multiplier and the blast zone the only way to win, which is the genre. That is
  a decision about what the mode is, not a defect, so it waits.

- ✔ **F0f** side and ceiling blast zones. The gate measured distance past the
  world AABB along `frame.down()` only, so a body knocked sideways died only
  once its arc happened to bring it below the world — which reads as the throw
  not having worked, and which is where a platform fighter loses MOST of its
  stocks.

  Both new margins are `Option` and absent by default, and that asymmetry is
  the design: the fall direction always kills, because every room has a pit
  whether it wanted one or not, but the sides mean OPPOSITE things in the two
  genres this engine has to serve. A platformer walking off the left edge of a
  corridor is transitioning to the next room; killing there would break every
  corridor in the game. A platform fighter thrown off the left edge has lost a
  stock. `None` says "this direction is not a blast zone", so nothing that
  exists today changes.

  Measured in the BODY's frame, so "ceiling" means "the way you do not fall"
  rather than "smaller y" — red-probed by swapping the ceiling test to screen
  space, which the up-gravity arm catches. Also probed by making the sides kill
  unconditionally, which the corridor case catches.

  ⚠ **And the app-level test was VACUOUS on its first draft.** It threw a
  fighter sideways at 1400px/s and gave it three seconds to die; it passed with
  the stage's side zone DISARMED, because gravity carried the body out through
  the FLOOR long before the window closed and the fall margin scored the kill.
  A test for a new blast direction that any other direction can satisfy proves
  nothing about the new one. Rewritten to park the body past the side margin
  but HIGH INSIDE the world and give it ten ticks — reaching the floor line from
  there takes about forty — plus an assertion that the corpse is still above the
  world when it dies. Second time today a red probe caught a weak test of mine
  rather than a bug (the first was "leaving the world is not the same event as
  touching a hazard", which asserted only the hazard half).

  Authorable the whole way down: `with_side_blast_margin` /
  `with_ceiling_blast_margin` in Rust, `side_blast_margin` /
  `ceiling_blast_margin` as LDtk level fields with defs registered in all five
  projects. The versus stage claims a 160px side zone — looser than its 96px
  floor, because you should be given further to recover from a horizontal
  launch than from a straight drop, a horizontal launch being the one you can
  act on. No ceiling zone there: nothing in the current moveset launches hard
  enough upward for one to be anything but a surprise.
- ✔ **F0g** LDtk can author a blast margin: a `blast_margin` level field in
  whole pixels, read into `RoomMetadata`, folded into the composed `World`.
  Zero is legal, negative is refused rather than clamped. **Registering the def
  turned up a second one**: `RoomMetadata::mode` — the field a hosted demo's
  entire ruleset gates on — documented itself as "authored as the LDtk level
  string field `mode`" while NO project declared it and no level set it. All
  five modes in the repo are assigned in Rust. Both defs are registered in all
  five `.ldtk` projects now, so the doc comment is true for the first time.
- ✔ **F0h** nothing defends against the edge of the world. `resolve_body_hit`
  takes an `unstoppable` argument that bypasses EVERY gate a body has —
  i-frames, the shield, a worn armor row, a wallet balance, and `never_dies` —
  because all of them are defences against BEING HIT and nothing hit a body
  that left the stage. The world stopped.

  The i-frame case is the one that mattered by the frame: the launch that
  throws a body off the stage is the SAME event that arms its knockback
  invulnerability (0.2s on an actor via `ACTOR_DAMAGE_IFRAME_S`, **0.75s** on
  the player via `feel.knockback_invulnerability_time`), so gating the blast
  zone on vulnerability failed hardest at exactly the case it exists for — up
  to 45 frames of falling past the line with nothing happening, then a death
  far below where it belonged.

  ⚠ **`never_dies` was worse than immortal, and that is a defect in what I
  shipped this morning.** A body that cannot be damaged to death, sitting past
  the blast line, is a PERMANENT EVENT SOURCE: the gate is a position test that
  re-fires every tick, `killed` was true for `LeftTheWorld` regardless, and
  health was never zeroed — so it re-triggered its own death once per frame,
  banner and all, forever. `sandbag_infinite` is the archetype that would have
  found it.

  The one gate `unstoppable` does NOT bypass is `!alive()`, and a test pins
  that too: without it, a corpse outside the world writes a lethal hit every
  frame for as long as it exists.

  Three production callers, and the third is honest about not knowing:
  `boss_hit` takes an `amount` and never sees the `HitEvent`, so it has no
  source to match on and passes `false` with the reason written down. Guessing
  `true` there would make every boss hit unblockable.

  Red-probed once per bypassed defence — remove the i-frame bypass, the shield
  bypass, or the `never_dies` bypass, and each fails its own case. The fixtures
  assert the ORDINARY hit is stopped first, so an arm that stopped defending
  would be caught rather than silently agreeing.

- ▢ **E1** the suite stays green, nothing uncommitted, no new `#[ignore]`
  without a triage doc naming what is ruled out.
  ✔ **21/21 at 2026-07-28** (1662s, full non-fast run). The job count moved from
  18: `repo tooling (scripts/tests)` and the two `web build check` jobs are new
  today, and both additions exist because something had been silently unrun —
  57 Python tests the cargo-only runner never executed (one of them RED for a
  day), and a wasm target nothing compiled (four errors, four days).
  ⚠ **"18/18" is a number that rots.** It is the job COUNT, and the count is
  supposed to grow. Stating the count rather than the property is how a green
  suite with a missing job reads as complete — which is exactly what both
  additions were. The property is *every planned job passes and the plan covers
  every target that ships*.
  ⚠ **"the suite" means `./run_tests.sh`, and 18/18 is ITS job count.**
  `cargo test --workspace` is a SUBSET: it does not compile feature-gated
  targets, so the demo shells' `visible` tests are invisible to it. Two of them
  were red for a day on 2026-07-27 while a workspace run reported 146 green
  blocks. `scripts/run_tests.py` computes per-crate feature jobs for exactly this
  reason — reach for `-p <crate> --features <f>` whenever a change touches
  anything gated.

## H. GPT-5.6 sixth review (2026-07-28, late) — rows I closed that were not closed

Jon handed over a review of the day's work. It is accurate on every claim I
could check, and the checkable ones were checked BEFORE any of this was
written. Six rows reopen; the two biggest are ones I closed hours earlier.

The recurring shape, in the review's own words: *"the lower-level mechanism
exists and its targeted harness is green, but the app or alternate construction
path still uses the older single-authority/single-player shape."* That is a
sharper statement of the same failure I spent all day finding in other people's
code — a capability that exists and a caller that does not use it.

- ✔ **H1 (their #3/#4) — CLOSED 2026-07-29 by the finalization barrier.**
  `prepare_character` produces `PreparedCharacterOverrides`, a type declared with
  NO visibility modifier inside `character_runtime::definition` — so the seated
  projection, seating and the worn path, all siblings of that module, physically
  cannot read a partial value. `CharacterPreparationPlugin::finish` folds the
  catalog in once for the whole cast and publishes complete definitions. Both
  paths read `PreparedKit`, whose two variants exist because exactly one case is
  undecidable before a body exists (the host kit is built from the body's own
  `AbilitySet`).
  ⚠ **the residue, stated rather than left to be found:** ids nothing REGISTERED
  still fold at wear time. That is most of the legacy cast, it has no prepared
  value to disagree with, and a seated fighter cannot be one — seating requires
  the registry. Both readers are named in the confinement contract.
  ⚠ **two surprises worth keeping.** `App::finish` re-runs EVERY plugin's
  `finish` on every call (it does not track which ran), so a barrier that
  consumes its input republished an EMPTY registry on the second call and the
  whole cast vanished. And the audit of hand-driven apps could not be an audit:
  the outlander fixture drives `update` by hand, published no cast, and its
  peaceful wanderer came out swinging the host protagonist's sword — so the
  barrier gained a `PreStartup` backstop calling the same idempotent finalizer.
  ⚠ **why nobody saw it.** Both shipped versus fighters author
  `ActionSet::default()` — `Some(empty)`, the Sanic case — so the arena never hit
  this path. The defect was real in the code and invisible in the product, which
  is exactly how it survived a playtest AND a closeout. A reader who finds this
  row and cannot reproduce it should learn that here rather than conclude the row
  was wrong.
  ⚠ the protagonist (`player`, `playable_kit: HostCode`) is not registered
  through `register_character` at all, so it takes the unregistered-catalog path
  and the fold did not change the highest-traffic body in the game.
  See [character-preparation-finalization-plan.md](character-preparation-finalization-plan.md).
  ✔ **Phase B slice 1 landed 2026-07-29:** the second kit writer is gone. The
  projection wrote seated bodies' action sets and movesets on the belief that
  seated bodies did not match `apply_worn_character_gameplay`.
  ⛔ **that belief was FALSE, and it had been repeated into this queue, a
  campaign doc, and the design of the projection itself.** `WornCharacter` is
  `#[require(IdentityKit)]` and `BodyAbilities` arrives with
  `AncillaryMovementBundle` — the two columns everyone said were missing were
  both present. Seated bodies always matched the single writer. The second writer
  was built on a diagnosis nobody re-read, and the diagnosis outlived the
  condition that made it true.
  The projection now serves only the population the derive genuinely cannot see:
  bodies with NO `WornCharacter`, identified by `CombatTuning.sprite_character_id`.
  The gate is that system's exact required-column set rather than a proxy —
  gating on `WornCharacter` would silently have meant "does it wear a character",
  which is a different question.
  ✔ `a_seated_fighter_inherits_the_kit_its_catalog_row_authors` is the H1 case
  itself, on the seated path, end to end. It could not have been written before
  Phase A.
  ⚠ the seating fixture did not contain the persona derive, so it proved whatever
  the OTHER writer happened to do. That is how this was missed for a day.
  ✔ **the `PreparedKit::HostCode` seated case is CLOSED, and by Phase B slice 1
  rather than by new work** — verified rather than assumed (2026-07-29). It needs
  the host kit built from that body's `AbilitySet`, which the derive has and the
  projection never did; routing seated bodies through the single writer closed it
  as a side effect. `a_seated_fighter_on_the_host_kit_is_not_left_empty_handed`
  drives it end to end and goes red when the derive is removed from the fixture,
  which is what proves the DERIVE is the thing serving it.

- ▢ **H1-old (superseded) — the "7 of 7" closeout is FALSE.**
  A catalog-playable character with no authored action set works as the worn
  player and gets an EMPTY kit when seated as player two or CPU.
  `seat_character` writes `ActionSet::default()` plus an empty `ActorMoveset` as
  placeholders; the projection that serves seated bodies writes only what the
  definition AUTHORED; so `None` — which the registry contract defines as "the
  catalog row stands" — leaves the placeholder standing instead.
  ⚠ my own comment on that branch names both cases and treats them as the same:
  *"whatever the body already carries stands (the catalog persona, or seating's
  placeholder)"*. They are not the same, and writing them in one parenthesis is
  how I missed it. Corrected in the inventory; the honest metric is **4 of 7**.
  → and identity REPLACEMENT is not atomic on the seated path: hurtboxes and
  movement tuning retract, the moveset / action set / combat kit / motion model
  do not, so a seated body changing character keeps the old kit for every field
  the new one left unauthored.

  ⚠ **the obvious fix is BLOCKED, and knowing why is most of the work.** The
  natural move — let `project_prepared_character_definitions` consult the
  catalog when the definition authored `None` — cannot be done: the workspace
  policy `engine.character-authority-is-app-local` forbids

  → **so the resolution belongs in PREPARATION, which is where X3 always said

  construction paths would project the same thing — the review's "one shared

  codebase already wanted.

  ⚠ and the trap on that road, checked rather than assumed: registration runs
  on `&mut App`, so the world IS reachable, but a provider plugin registering

  row and bake the absence permanently. The `None` vs `Some(empty)` distinction
  must also survive the fold — `Some(empty)` stays empty, only `None` takes the
  row — or Sanic gets a punch. This is a composition-order design decision, not
  a mechanical edit, and it is the last real piece of Campaign 1.
- ✔ **H2 (their #5) — an authored ranged action set derives a ranged move.**
  `build_actor_moveset(None, set.melee.as_ref(), None, special)` hard-codes the
  ranged argument to `None` while `set.ranged` may exist. The body then

  timeline to execute. Preparation validates only the reverse mismatch.
  ⚠ this is what X6 claimed to have closed.
  ✔ **FIXED 2026-07-28, and the review's fix needed narrowing.** Passing
  `set.ranged.as_ref()` unconditionally — which is what the review proposes —
  turned an existing test red, and that test was right to go red: the HOST-CODE
  kit already fires ranged through the legacy projectile path
  (`charges_projectiles`), so folding a moveset ranged verb in would make one
  press do two things. The gate is therefore the opposite way round from the
  `special` fold two lines above it: `special` is folded ONLY for the host-code
  kit, `ranged` only for everyone else. `charges_projectiles` is precisely the
  question "does this kit already have a way to fire", so it is the condition
  rather than a proxy for one.
  → `an_authored_ranged_action_set_derives_a_ranged_move`, red-probed by
  restoring the hard-coded `None`; 1085 actors tests green.
  ✔ **H2b — `RangedExecution` is the sole switch now, and finding that out
  found a hole in H2's own coverage.** The plan filed this as "useful only if it
  becomes the sole switch; otherwise it is a third name for the same implicit
  split", so the first job was checking whether it could be. It could: HOW a body
  fires decides four things, and they were being decided in three places in two
  spellings —
  a `bool` named after the fourth (`charges_projectiles`), a hard-coded `None`
  for ranged in the host-code builder, and a hard-coded `None` for special at the
  catalog site, each carrying a comment explaining it was the opposite of the
  other. A fifth consultation could have been written in a third spelling without
  contradicting anything.
  → `enum RangedExecution { HostCharge, MovesetVerb }` now decides all four:
  which presets fold, whether the protagonist's blade SFX is stamped, and whether
  the body carries `ChargesProjectiles`. The two moveset builders collapse into
  one `derive_persona_moveset(set, execution, authored)` — they had differed only
  in what this enum says.
  → ⚠ **and the red probe found H2 half-covered.** Reinstating the exact defect
  H2 closed — `MovesetVerb => (None, None)` — left all 1101 actors tests GREEN.
  `an_authored_ranged_action_set_derives_a_ranged_move` drives a REGISTERED
  character, whose fold happens at the preparation barrier; the unregistered
  catalog cast, which is most of the content, derives separately and had no test.
  The row read as covered because a test with the right name existed.
  → `an_unregistered_authored_persona_derives_its_ranged_move` closes it,
  red-probed the same way. 1102 actors tests green.
  → the opposite probe (`HostCharge` folding ranged) reddens
  `host_code_kit_refreshes_when_body_abilities_change`, so both arms are pinned.
- ✔ **H3 (their #1) — production GGRS starts the players who are here.**
  `rollback_observatory.rs` builds `SyncTestSettings { .., ..Default::default() }`
  and the default player count is 1. The multi-handle API and the two-stream
  harness are real; the production composition does not use them. Y1 reopens —
  and the queue was internally contradictory, since Y2's own note says seats 1–3
  are not in the session while Y1 says they are.
  → second half: `ambition_host` installs `SlotControlLatches` only under
  `sim_is_fixed_tick()`, which recognizes `FixedUpdate` and not the GGRS
  schedule, so the visible GGRS host has no secondary latch at all.
  ⚠ **and I read this half wrong before fixing it.** I concluded NEITHER latch
  was installed under GGRS; the review said only the primary was, and the review
  was right — `rollback_observatory` already registers
  `accumulate_control_frame_latch` and owns the primary. Acting on my reading
  installed a SECOND copy, and `no_system_is_registered_twice_in_one_schedule`
  caught it with the exact words the guard exists to say: *"2x Update
  accumulate_control_frame_latch"*. The gap was the SEATS only.
  ✔ **and the first attempt broke four rollback oracles**, which is the more
  useful failure. `capture_latched_local_input` did `pending.0 = latch.take()`
  unconditionally, so the moment a latch EXISTED it overwrote whatever a driver
  had written — and every rollback harness drives `PendingLocalInput` directly.
  An empty latch means "no device spoke", not "the device said nothing". The
  latch now carries `was_fed` and only claims the frame when a device has fed
  it. That is the same lesson as `drive_control_frame` clearing
  `PendingSeatInputs` this morning: silence is not a request.
- ✔ **H4 (their #2) — hot reload keeps the session it reloads.**
  The LDtk rebase reconstructs `SyncTestSettings` with `..Default::default()`,
  preserving `max_prediction_window` and dropping `players`. Even once H3 lands,
  a reload silently removes the secondary handles.
- ✔ **H3b (round 2) — ONE FROZEN SEAT TOPOLOGY.** The roster and the rollback
  session both sampled `LocalDeviceOrder` independently, so a controller
  connecting between the two samples made them disagree while both cited "the
  connected controllers" — the roster seating a fighter the session has no
  handle for. `LocalSeatTopology` is captured ONCE when the session starts, and
  the roster reads the snapshot when a session exists (the live order still
  answers for the shell's non-rollback routes, where there is no session to
  disagree with).
  → its generation advances on every capture INCLUDING an identical one, for
  the same reason `CharacterCatalogGeneration` does: two identical decisions at
  different times are still two decisions.
  → 4 tests, behind the `input` feature: a mid-session connection does not add a
  seat, zero devices is still one player, handles map in connection order, and
  a re-capture is a new generation.
- ✔ **H3c (round 3) — the RULE that kept H3 and H4 fixed was a doc comment, so
  the compiler keeps it now instead.** Re-reading H3/H4 to close them found all
  four session-construction sites correct and this sitting above them, on the
  field itself:

  > ⚠ **every construction of this struct must set it.** `..Default::default()`
  > silently means one player[…]

  A load-bearing negative that nothing enforced — the exact shape this campaign
  spent two days replacing with mechanical barriers, and it had already failed
  three times running (initial session, hot-reload rebase, proof-pulse restore,
  each repaired individually while the next site stayed free to make the same
  mistake). Three identical bugs is not three accidents.
  → `impl Default for SyncTestSettings` is **deleted**.
  `SyncTestSettings::for_players(n)` replaces it, and a caller that has not
  decided how many people are playing cannot type it.
  → ⛔ **and the obvious version of this would have been wrong.** Deleting
  `Default` outright breaks an argument `fixtures/external_consumer` makes
  deliberately, in a comment: a THIRD PARTY should name only the fields it cares
  about, because spelling every field out is how a consumer breaks when the
  engine adds one. That fixture is the repo's instrument for exactly that, and it
  was right. The split that satisfies both is **tuning vs topology**:
  `check_distance` and `max_prediction_window` are tuning and a default is a real
  answer; how many people are playing is not tuning and has no honest default. So
  the spread survives and its BASE asks. Outlander now says it is single-player.
  → the hot-reload rebase becomes `SyncTestSettings { check_distance: 0,
  ..settings }` — inheriting from the session it replaces, so PRESERVATION is the
  default and dropping something is what you have to type. `players:
  settings.players` had repaired one field and left the shape that caused it;
  "who is playing" was not the last topology this struct will ever carry.
  → and the compiler immediately found **two sites the grep had missed**, both in
  `dev_runtime`'s own hot-reload tests — including one inside the test named
  `a_reload_keeps_every_local_player_in_the_session`, which set `players: 4` and
  then spread a default across the rest. No new test: the property is a compile
  error now, and a test asserting `for_players(3).players == 3` would be a test
  of the language.

### The architecture decision, argued and settled (2026-07-28)

**→ THE PLAN LIVES IN
[character-preparation-finalization-plan.md](character-preparation-finalization-plan.md).**
Three rounds of argument, three refusals accepted, an execution order that keeps
a bisect possible, and the caveats that only surfaced in round three (`finish`
vs `cleanup`, manually-driven apps needing explicit finalization, hot reload as
a separate transaction, transactional publication). The summary below is the
ledger's pointer; the doc is the detail.

The reviewer proposed a `PreparedBodyBlueprint` + a `BodyUnderConstruction`
marker + a `PreparedMatchPlan`. I pushed back on three points and the reviewer
accepted all three; what follows is the settled shape, recorded because it is
the plan for the rest of Campaign 1.

- ⛔ **No new stored authority.** A blueprint carrying action set / moveset /
  motion / hurtboxes / provider beside `PreparedCharacterDefinition` is R-g's
  seventh authority with a new name. A borrowed view or a function return is
  fine; a stored one is not.
- ⛔ **No `BodyUnderConstruction` marker.** Its safety is `Without<..>` in every
  present and future body query — the identical shape to
  `apply_worn_character_gameplay` silently not matching seated bodies (which IS
  H1's root) and to the touch test querying `TouchActionButton` while the d-pad
  was not one. The rule is "do not spawn until complete"; where construction
  must be deferred, defer a REQUEST rather than publishing an incomplete body.
- ⛔ **Rollback cannot sample device discovery.** A plan assembled from live
  devices inside a rollback tick does not reproduce on resimulation. External
  topology is frozen BEFORE the session; only deterministic selections and
  activation phase live inside it.
- ⚠ **and Choice B is not available for the path that is broken.** The
  reviewer's intermediate — one resolver taking `(&prepared, &catalog)` — works
  for the WORN path, which already holds the catalog, and cannot reach the
  SEATED path: `project_prepared_character_definitions` is engine-side and
  `engine.character-authority-is-app-local` forbids it a `CharacterCatalog`.
  So the real choice is A (a complete prepared type published after
  composition) or C (mutate and seal the existing registry).
- ✔ **A, and for the reason that already settled the marker.** C's safety
  condition is "no code reads the registry before sealing" — a load-bearing
  negative nothing enforces, spread over every present and future reader. A can
  be COMPILER-enforced: if the partial type is `pub(crate)` or lives where
  runtime cannot import it, the phase split is mechanical. Two types with the
  same visibility would just be the blueprint objection wearing a new name, so
  that visibility IS the acceptance test.
- ✔ **and Bevy already owns the barrier this needs: `Plugin::finish`.** It runs
  after every plugin's `build()`, it exists in `bevy_app` 0.18.1, and this
  workspace has **zero** uses of it. That answers the ordering hazard recorded
  above — a provider registering its cast before the app installs
  `CharacterCatalog` would fold in an empty row and bake the absence
  permanently — with a mechanical hook instead of a documented ordering rule.

- ✔ **H5 (their #6) — the countdown WAITS for the roster, and nobody acts before it.**
  `settle_versus_round` took a `MatchSeated` param and holds `Starting` at its
  full count until every participant has a body. Held rather than paused
  mid-count: a countdown that starts, stalls and resumes reads as a stutter, and
  the fighters are held by `ScriptedControl` anyway.
  ⚠ **tested as a PREDICATE, and the reason is itself a finding.** My first
  test drove the real app, forced `MatchSeated(false)` and asserted the count
  held. It FAILED — seating re-asserts `MatchSeated(true)` every tick once the
  roster is complete, so poking the resource is corrected before the next
  update and proves nothing. Reaching the real case needs a participant that
  CANNOT seat, which the fixture cannot yet produce. `countdown_may_advance` is
  tested directly instead, including that `None` — no seating system at all —
  RELEASES, because a countdown that can never start is worse than one that
  starts early.
  ▢ **the FIRST-TICK half is not done.** `ScriptedControl` is requested in
  `CombatSet::Settle`, after input, move triggering, playback, projectile
  materialization and damage have already run for that tick, and command
  insertion is deferred on top — so a newly seated fighter can process one full
  simulation tick before suspension applies. The fix is to seat fighters ALREADY
  suspended rather than to gate them a tick later, which is the same
  "spawn-complete" rule as
  [character-preparation-finalization-plan.md](character-preparation-finalization-plan.md)
  and belongs with that work rather than beside it.
  ✔ **the first-tick half LANDED as `opens_suspended`**, and this row went on
  describing it as open. The roster declares that a match opens suspended and
  seating applies `ScriptedControl` in `PlayerInputSet::CharacterProjection` — so
  it is a property of how a fighter is SEATED, not a gate applied a tick later,
  which is exactly what this row asked for.
  → and it lands on the same tick for a real schedule reason, not by luck: Bevy's
  `auto_insert_apply_deferred` is on (this workspace disables it in exactly one
  test schedule), so seating's commands flush before `PlayerInputSet::ControlGate`
  — the phase scripted blanking runs in — within the same update.
  ⚠ **but its test covered the easy half.** Two SPAWNED fighters cannot act before
  their suspension lands almost by construction: the body does not exist until the
  same command queue flushes. Seat 0 is different — it ADOPTS the primary player,
  a body that already exists, is already in the brain's query, and was accepting
  input on the tick before. That is the body "a held direction carried in from the
  menu" was about, and it was the one nothing asked about.
  → `an_adopted_seat_is_also_suspended_on_the_tick_it_joins` — green, and red when
  the `opens_suspended` block is disabled. The property held; it was unpinned.

- ▢ **H5-old (superseded, kept for the symptom) — the versus countdown can finish before the roster is
  seated.** `settle_versus_round` decrements the starting countdown as soon as
  the roster and geometry exist and never reads `MatchSeated`, so the round can
  go live before every fighter has a body, and a late participant joins a live
  round without the countdown/reset boundary. There is also a one-tick control
  gap: `ScriptedControl` is requested in `CombatSet::Settle`, after input,
  triggering, playback and damage for that tick.
- ✔ **H6 (their #7) — CLOSED 2026-07-29.** `ProjectedCharacterKit` carries
  `{ id, generation }` and a mismatch in EITHER re-projects.
  ⚠ **the comparison was only half of it, and the half that is easy to write.**
  With the generation stamped and compared, the probe still failed: a cast
  replacement changes NOTHING on a body, so the `Changed<WornCharacter>` query
  never saw it. The system now walks all bodies on the tick the registry
  changes — startup and hot reload — and keeps the change-detection fast path
  otherwise. Verified by removing each half separately and watching the probe go
  red both times; a green guard on a clean tree proves nothing.
  ⚠ **a latent bug the barrier introduced, found here.** The generation used to
  advance per INSERT. The barrier publishes the whole cast at once, so a rebuilt
  registry — a fresh `Default` — would have restarted the counter and republished
  generation 1 over a body stamped with generation 1 from the PREVIOUS cast.
  Every staleness check would have read "still current". A monotonic counter that
  restarts is worse than no counter, because it looks like one. It is one
  generation per PUBLICATION now, carried across rebuilds.

- ▢ **H6-old (superseded) — `CharacterCatalogGeneration` invalidates nothing.** X4 is
  marked done and no production code reads `generation()`; the only readers are
  tests. `ProjectedCharacterKit` stores just the character id, so a body early-
  exits on an unchanged id and cannot know its derived kit is stale after the
  cast is replaced. Either stamp projections with the generation or withdraw
  the completion claim.
- ✔ **H7 (their #8) — the guards now say what they prove.** Renamed to
  `*-is-confined-to-one-file`, with the distinction stated once above the table:
  excluding the allowed file proves no reference exists OUTSIDE it, not that
  exactly one call exists inside — `provider_of_character` already has two calls
  in `presentation.rs` and satisfies its guard. Confinement is genuinely what
  stops a SECOND file growing its own opinion, which is how every
  split-authority bug in this campaign started; it is just not singularity, and
  Campaign 1 cited it as if it were. If exact counts ever matter, the note says
  to add a positive assertion inside the allowed file rather than rename back.
  ⚠ this includes the contract I added TODAY, whose prose said "one caller"
  while I had already noticed the two calls and rationalised them as "one file".

- ▢ **H7-old (superseded) — the one-caller contracts prove CONFINEMENT, not
  singularity**, including the one I added today. They exclude the whole allowed
  file, so two calls inside it satisfy a guard whose prose says "one caller" —
  and `provider_of_character` already has exactly that. I noticed the two calls
  when I wrote the row and rationalised it as "one file"; the prose still says
  caller. Either rename to confinement or assert an exact count inside the file.


## Y′. GPT-5.6 handoff review (2026-07-29) — the topology froze the easy half

Jon handed over a fresh review mid-Phase-A. Every claim I could check was
checkable and correct, which is worth recording as calibration: the previous
round contained one attribution the reviewer later acknowledged as context
contamination, and this one contained none.

The theme is the same one H1 was about, aimed at a different seam: a mechanism
was made a single authority in NAME on 2026-07-28 while its consumers kept
sampling something else.

- ✔ **Y′1 (their #1, CRITICAL) — completing a proof pulse reset the session to
  one player.** The baseline restore rebuilt `SyncTestSettings` from
  prediction-window values with `..Default::default()`, whose `players` is 1. A
  four-player match came back from F9 with one GGRS handle while the frozen
  topology and the versus roster both still said four. The SAME defect that was
  fixed for initial startup and for hot reload, surviving in the third path that
  constructs a session — which is the argument for finding every construction
  path before calling a row closed, not the first two.
- ✔ **Y′2 (their #3) — the topology was recaptured for every GGRS sub-session.**
  Baseline and proof are one gameplay session restarted; recapturing made the
  topology stable per SUB-session, a lifetime nothing else uses. Frozen once per
  gameplay session now, and RELEASED when gameplay ends — the roster reads any
  frozen topology without asking whether a session owns it, so a stale one meant
  two people who played, quit and came back with one pad still seated two.
- ✔ **Y′3 (their #2) — `assign_local_seat_devices` still read live
  `LocalDeviceOrder`.** Freezing the handle COUNT and not the handle→device
  MAPPING is freezing the half that is easy to test. It reads the frozen topology
  while one is active. The test asserts the unintuitive half: handle 0 keeps
  pointing at its absent controller and reads nothing, because promoting the next
  pad into that seat is the silent reassignment being prevented.
- ✔ **Y′4 (their #4) — `SlotControlLatches` survived session replacement.** The
  primary latch was reset and the seat latches were not, so after a hot reload or
  a proof pulse player one started clean while players two through four could
  carry a jump edge from the previous timeline. The whole input-authority cluster
  is replaced atomically now, on rebase AND on stop. The preserved one was the
  half a single-player test could never notice.
- ✔ **Y′5 (their #5) — route capture could report success having adopted no
  camera.** Warmup is a duration; readiness is a fact. It waits for an adopted
  camera and fails loudly, naming the route, if none arrives.
- ✔ **Y′6 (their #6) — the old campaign doc still told a closed-campaign story.**
  Marked SUPERSEDED at the top, pointing at the canonical status. Dangerous
  precisely at compaction. Its X3 row argued AGAINST resolving at preparation
  time; that objection is answered rather than dropped — right about eager
  resolution, wrong that preparation had to mean eager.
- ✔ **Y′7 — two stale comments, corrected.** `SyncTestSettings::default` no
  longer claims the shipped host installs one player;
  `publish_latched_slot_controls` no longer says seats 1-3 are absent from the
  session. Both described the architecture as it was before the fix, which is how
  a repair gets aimed at the wrong place.

### Still open from this review

- ✔ **Y′8 (their H5 half) — CLOSED 2026-07-29.** `MatchParticipantRoster` carries
  `opens_suspended`, and seating applies `ScriptedControl` in the SAME command
  flush that creates the bodies — so a fighter is never observable in a state the
  ruleset did not ask for. The window is closed rather than narrowed, which is
  the difference between a fix and a smaller bug.
  ⚠ the flag lives on the ROSTER, not on seating's own judgement. A stage with no
  countdown opens live, and a second test asserts exactly that: seating deciding
  on its own that a fighter cannot act would be the engine holding an opinion the
  ruleset owns.
  ⚠ the probe asserts after ONE update. "Suspended by the time the fight starts"
  would have passed against the bug, because the bug WAS one tick.
- ✔ **Y′9 — the roster is stamped, reconciled, activated, and now REPAIRED.**
  `MatchParticipantRoster.seat_topology` records which frozen topology decided
  its seat count, and `reconcile_roster_with_frozen_topology` compares them.
  The hole this closes is real and ordinary, not contrived: a route is entered —
  and its roster built from LIVE device discovery — before its rollback session
  exists, so a controller connecting in that gap left the roster seating two
  fighters into a three-handle session with both citing "the connected
  controllers".
  ⚠ **before seating a roster is only an INTENTION**, so it is rebuilt against
  the topology that won. **After seating it is bodies on a stage** and it is
  reported, not silently reseated — reseating mid-match is a worse bug than the
  disagreement.
  ▢ **what remains is match ACTIVATION**, which is what would make the
  post-seating case impossible rather than merely loud: validate every
  participant, activate the roster atomically, publish it, and start the
  countdown from THAT. Written up in
  [character-preparation-finalization-plan.md](character-preparation-finalization-plan.md)
  under "Related, deliberately after". Building it inside a change about a
  counter would have been the wrong place for it.
  ✔ **activation landed** (`ActiveMatch`, see Y″5), and with it the repair this
  row had to leave as a warning.
  ✔ **and the repairable half turns out to be the COMMON one.** A live match
  disagreeing with its session sounds like a fighter arriving mid-round; the
  ordinary case is nothing of the kind. The route builds its roster from live
  device discovery on entry, stamped `None` because no session has frozen
  anything yet; the fighters seat; the rollback session starts and freezes its
  topology. Now the roster disagrees with the session about which decision
  produced it while agreeing completely about who is fighting — and the
  reconciler warned, about a stage where nothing was wrong.
  → `ActiveMatch::adopt_seat_topology` is the one legitimate mutation of a live
  activation, and it is narrow on purpose: it can record which topology decided a
  seating, and it cannot move a body, add a seat or drop one. A real disagreement
  — different fighters — is still reported and still not reseated.
  → ⚠ **the obvious comparison is WRONG, and the probe proved it.** A one-player
  topology seats TWO participants (there is always an opponent, human or CPU), so
  `participants.len() == topology.players()` reports a disagreement in the
  ordinary single-player case. Substituting it reddens the new test. The
  comparison is between the FIGHTERS the frozen topology would build and the
  fighters that are live, which is what "the same match" means.
  → `a_live_match_that_agrees_with_its_session_adopts_its_topology`, red-probed
  twice (no repair; wrong comparison).

## AA. GPT-5.6 review round 3 (2026-07-29) — the boundaries of "complete"

Handed over after the `RangedExecution` and topology work. Its framing is the
useful part: *"the remaining defects sit exactly at the boundaries now described
as complete"*. Every finding was checked against the code before acting; five of
six held, one is overstated and is argued below rather than accepted.

- ✔ **AA5 (their #5) — and it CLOSED Z′14 after six of my measurements did not.**
  `capture_scene` carried its own copy of the asset-root rule reading
  `crates/ambition::actors/assets`. See Z′14 for the full row. The lesson is not
  the typo: a verification TOOL is the worst possible place for a second copy of
  a rule, because its whole job is to tell you what is on screen.
- ✔ **AA1 (their #1, HIGH) — `CombatKit` was a second, stale identity baseline.**
  Seating seeds it from `ActionSet::default()` — empty, matching what an enemy
  spawn does before its archetype fills one in — and `apply_worn_character_
  gameplay` then installed the real action set, moveset and identity kit without
  touching it. `CombatKit` is not decoration: its own doc calls it the durable
  source of "what the actor can do innately", and `apply_catalog_mode` (a brain
  command), the mount pair and autonomous reconciliation all REBUILD an
  `ActionSet` from it. A seated fighter could act through its live set and lose
  its innate attacks the moment anything rebuilt them.
  → published by the one persona writer, beside `IdentityKit`, from the same
  resolved set. `Option<&mut CombatKit>` because not every body has one — a
  seated fighter does, the plain player bundle does not, and "no second baseline"
  is a different claim from "leave it stale".
  → `a_seated_fighter_keeps_one_capability_baseline_not_two`, red-probed.
- ✔ **AA3 (their #3, HIGH) — the same character was a different BODY in each seat.**
  A spawned seat sized itself from `BodySource::Explicit` and took
  `prepared.vitals.mass`; the adopted primary player kept whatever box and weight
  its session gave it. A mirror match put two different shapes and two different
  masses on the stage, and the wrong one was always player one.
